It’s hard to separate, because restaurants are already loud and rushed and people want things from you every second, so sometimes I think, “Well, of course I’m tense.”\n\nBut if you mean am I sitting around every day feeling panicky or scared for no reason, I don’t think so. Like if I mess something up, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why can’t I just be normal about things?” But I don’t think I’ve had this big heavy feeling that I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more like… things don’t line up right in my head for a while, and I’m aware people are around me, but I’m not connecting to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike, if you mean do I look around and think, “This isn’t real,” I don’t think so. I don’t remember thinking that in a clear way. But I can feel kind of far away from what’s happening, like there’s a delay between what I see and what I’m supposed to do next. People might be talking to me, and I hear them, but it doesn’t always land the way it should.\n\nSo maybe detached a little, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as being in a dream. It’s not like I’m floating above myself or anything dramatic like that. It’s more confusing and stuck, like I’m there, but not fully keeping up with the room.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes been affecting your relationships with coworkers or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because I keep wanting to say it’s just work stress or people are overreacting, but it’s not just that.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten to the point where I’m pretty sure I’m close to losing the shift-lead opportunity they were talking to me about. Before all this, my manager had been kind of testing me out with more responsibility, like helping with closing and keeping things moving during service. And now it feels like every time I make one of those mistakes, or I get confused and hold people up, it becomes this reason not to trust me with it.\n\nIt’s not even one big dramatic thing every time. It’s little things that really matter in a restaurant — closing checks wrong, slowing down service, forgetting what step I’m supposed to do next, getting stuck when everyone else needs me to move faster. Then other people have to cover for me, or someone has to come behind me and fix something, and I can tell they’re frustrated. They don’t always say it directly, but you can feel it. Like people start watching you differently.\n\nWith coworkers, I think some of them are still nice to me, but it’s awkward now. I feel like I’ve become someone they have to manage around instead of rely on. And that hurts because I used to be good at that job. I liked being the person who could handle a rush.\n\nOutside of work… mostly it’s my husband. He says I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’ll be sitting there at home, but not really with him, if that makes sense. He’ll tell me about his day or ask me something, and I guess I don’t respond right away, or I seem like I’m somewhere else. He’s been patient, but I can tell he’s tired and lonely in it. And then I feel guilty, because I’m physically there, but I’m not giving him much of me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're not in control of your actions during these episodes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"What scared everyone at work is that I started freezing up in the middle of service or doing things out of order, like standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key and repeating table numbers instead of closing checks.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… not exactly like somebody else is controlling me, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I’m not controlling myself the way I normally would. Like my body is there and I’m holding the thing I need, but I’m not doing the next step.

At work, that’s what scared people, I think. I’d freeze in the middle of service when everything was moving fast, or I’d start doing things in the wrong order. I remember standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and I was supposed to be using it to close checks, but instead I was just repeating table numbers to myself. I knew they were table numbers, and I knew I was at work, but I wasn’t making myself move on to what I was supposed to do.

So it’s not like I black out and wake up somewhere else. It’s more embarrassing than that, almost, because I’m partly aware that I’m messing up, but I can’t seem to organize myself into the right action fast enough. Like I get stuck in one step while everyone else is already three steps ahead.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work kind of made me come in. I’m a waitress, and things have been getting weird during service, not just like normal stress or being busy. What scared everyone was that I started freezing up in the middle of a shift, like I’d just stop with people waiting and food needing to go out.\n\nAnd I’d do things in the wrong order, too. I still know where I am, I just don’t feel like I’m connecting to it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I get stressed like anybody does, especially at work or if I’m running late or there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know if I’d call it being anxious all the time or constantly on edge.\n\nWhen I’m not having one of those moments, I can still feel a little keyed up sometimes, like I’m kind of watching myself to see if I’m going to mess up or if something weird is going to happen, but I don’t know how often that is compared to normal stress. It’s hard to separate, because restaurants are already loud and rushed and people want things from you every second, so sometimes I think, “Well, of course I’m tense.”\n\nBut if you mean am I sitting around every day feeling panicky or scared for no reason, I don’t think so. Like if I mess something up, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why can’t I just be normal about things?” But I don’t think I’ve had this big heavy feeling that I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more like self-criticism, I guess.\n\nSo… not really, not lately in a major way. Nothing major or traumatic recently. I knew they were table numbers, but I wasn’t doing what I was supposed to be doing with them, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd in a restaurant, everything is so fast, so if you stop or get mixed up, people notice immediately. Almost like when you’re really tired and everything feels kind of flat or separate from you, except it happens when I’m supposed to be working and responding.\n\nSo… maybe a little unreal, but not completely unreal. I still know where I am, I just don’t feel like I’m connecting to it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I get stressed like anybody does, especially at work or if I’m running late or there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know if I’d call it being anxious all the time or constantly on edge.\n\nWhen I’m not having one of those moments, I can still feel a little keyed up sometimes, like I’m kind of watching myself to see if I’m going to mess up or if something weird is going to happen, but I don’t know how often that is compared to normal stress. It’s hard to separate, because restaurants are already loud and rushed and people want things from you every second, so sometimes I think, “Well, of course I’m tense.”\n\nBut if you mean am I sitting around every day feeling panicky or scared for no reason, I don’t think so. Not usually. I have times where I feel fine, or at least normal for me. So… maybe some stress, maybe some nervousness, but I haven’t really noticed it as its own separate thing the way I notice the episodes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to say yes or no like I know for sure.\n\nWith appetite, I think it’s mostly just… normal? Some days at work I forget to eat because it gets busy, or I’ll just pick at something late, but that’s kind of always been how restaurant shifts go for me. I haven’t noticed a big change where food suddenly seems disgusting or I’m starving all the time or anything like that.\n\nSleep is harder to answer because my schedule can be weird, and if I get home late it takes me a while to settle down. But I don’t know if that’s new or if it’s just my routine being inconsistent. I might have a few nights where I don’t sleep great, then I’ll crash more on another night. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern recently, I guess. If you want, I can try to pay more attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of worthlessness or guilt about yourself lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around thinking, “I’m worthless,” in those exact words. That feels too strong, maybe. I do get frustrated with myself sometimes, especially if I feel like I’m not handling things the way I should or if I’m slower than other people at work, but I don’t know if that’s guilt or just being annoyed with myself.\n\nGuilt… I mean, maybe in small ways? Like if I mess something up, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why can’t I just be normal about things?” But I don’t think I’ve had this big heavy feeling that I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more like self-criticism, I guess.\n\nSo… not really, not lately in a major way. I haven’t noticed worthlessness or guilt as a big thing on its own.\n\nDoctor: Has something very stressful or upsetting happened to you recently, like the loss of a loved one or a traumatic experience?\n\nPatient: Um… not that I can think of. I mean, nothing like someone close to me dying or an accident or being attacked or anything like that. I’ve had stressful days, definitely, and things can build up, especially with work and normal life stuff, but I don’t think there’s been one big event where I’d say, “That’s what set everything off.”\n\nI keep trying to think if I’m forgetting something, because sometimes when people ask that I feel like maybe there’s supposed to be an answer, but… no, I don’t think so. Nothing major or traumatic recently. Just regular stress, I guess, not something like a loss or a specific terrible thing happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so? Like, I’ve heard people describe that as feeling like they’re floating above themselves or watching themselves like a movie, and I don’t really recognize that, at least not clearly.\n\nSometimes when I’m stressed or tired I can feel kind of disconnected, like I’m going through the motions or not fully paying attention, but I wouldn’t say I’m outside my body looking at myself. It’s more like I’m in my own head too much, if that makes sense. I’m still aware I’m there, I’m still the one doing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that specific feeling. If you mean something more subtle than that, maybe I’d need you to explain what you’re asking, because I might be misunderstanding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I do worry, but I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a way that stands out. Like, I worry about normal things—whether I’m going to mess something up at work, or if I said something weird, or if I forgot to do something—but it’s hard for me to tell what’s normal worry versus something bigger.\n\nWhen I’m not having one of those episodes, I can usually sort of keep going. The worry might be in the background, like a little noise, but it’s not always right in my face. Some days I’m calmer, and some days I get stuck thinking through things too much. I’ll replay conversations or think ahead about what could go wrong, but I don’t know if that’s “often” or just me being kind of cautious.\n\nSo… I guess yes, sometimes I worry when I’m not in one of those episodes, but I haven’t really kept track of it. It’s not like I wake up every day thinking, “I’m worried,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the time, no. I mean, I get anxious, but it’s hard for me to measure it like that. If I’m at work and it’s busy, or I feel like someone is annoyed with me, then yeah, I can get tense and start worrying. And sometimes even when nothing is actually happening, I’ll have this background feeling like I should be paying attention to something, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nBut if you mean every day, all day, like I’m constantly anxious even when nothing else is going on… I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly. There are times where I feel okay, or at least distracted enough that I’m not really noticing anxiety. Then other times I’ll realize I’ve been clenching my jaw or overthinking something small for way too long.\n\nSo… I guess I do feel anxious sometimes outside of the episodes, but I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having another one of these episodes happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about that. Maybe not constantly in a neat way, like “I’m afraid of an episode,” but I worry about it happening at work again because that’s where it got really embarrassing and scary for other people.\n\nWhat scared everyone was I started sort of freezing up in the middle of service. Like I’d be standing there when I was supposed to be closing checks or running drinks, and I just… wouldn’t move on to the next thing right. Or I’d do things out of order. One time I was by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and instead of going to close checks, I was just repeating table numbers to myself. I knew they were table numbers, but I wasn’t doing what I was supposed to be doing with them, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd in a restaurant, everything is so fast, so if you stop or get mixed up, people notice immediately. Servers are asking where the key is, customers are waiting, managers are looking at you like, “What are you doing?” So yes, I worry about it happening again, especially when we’re busy, because I don’t always feel like I’ll catch it before other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a dream or detached from the world around you when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t know if I’d call it feeling like I’m in a dream, exactly. It’s more like… things don’t line up right in my head for a while, and I’m aware people are around me, but I’m not connecting to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike, if you mean do I look around and think, “This isn’t real,” I don’t think so. I don’t remember thinking that in a clear way. But I can feel kind of far away from what’s happening, like there’s a delay between what I see and what I’m supposed to do next. People might be talking to me, and I hear them, but it doesn’t always land the way it should.\n\nSo maybe detached a little, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as being in a dream. It’s not like I’m floating above myself or anything dramatic like that. It’s more confusing and stuck, like I’m there, but not fully keeping up with the room.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes been affecting your relationships with coworkers or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because I keep wanting to say it’s just work stress or people are overreacting, but it’s not just that.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten to the point where I’m pretty sure I’m close to losing the shift-lead opportunity they were talking to me about. Before all this, my manager had been kind of testing me out with more responsibility, like helping with closing and keeping things moving during service. And now it feels like every time I make one of those mistakes, or I get confused and hold people up, it becomes this reason not to trust me with it.\n\nIt’s not even one big dramatic thing every time. It’s little things that really matter in a restaurant — closing checks wrong, slowing down service, forgetting what step I’m supposed to do next, getting stuck when everyone else needs me to move faster. Then other people have to cover for me, or someone has to come behind me and fix something, and I can tell they’re frustrated. They don’t always say it directly, but you can feel it. Like people start watching you differently.\n\nWith coworkers, I think some of them are still nice to me, but it’s awkward now. I feel like I’ve become someone they have to manage around instead of rely on. And that hurts because I used to be good at that job. I liked being the person who could handle a rush.\n\nOutside of work… mostly it’s my husband. He says I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’ll be sitting there at home, but not really with him, if that makes sense. He’ll tell me about his day or ask me something, and I guess I don’t respond right away, or I seem like I’m somewhere else. He’s been patient, but I can tell he’s tired and lonely in it. And then I feel guilty, because I’m physically there, but I’m not giving him much of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not in control of your actions during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not exactly like somebody else is controlling me, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I’m not controlling myself the way I normally would. Like my body is there and I’m holding the thing I need, but I’m not doing the next step.\n\nAt work, that’s what scared people, I think. I’d freeze in the middle of service when everything was moving fast, or I’d start doing things in the wrong order. I remember standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and I was supposed to be using it to close checks, but instead I was just repeating table numbers to myself. I knew they were table numbers, and I knew I was at work, but I wasn’t making myself move on to what I was supposed to do.\n\nSo it’s not like I black out and wake up somewhere else. It’s more embarrassing than that, almost, because I’m partly aware that I’m messing up, but I can’t seem to organize myself into the right action fast enough. Like I get stuck in one step while everyone else is already three steps ahead.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah. I mean… yes, definitely. That’s honestly the part that makes it harder to keep telling myself it’s just me being tired or having a bad week.

At work it’s been a real problem. I’m a waitress, and before this got worse, they were talking about maybe moving me toward a shift-lead kind of role, or at least letting me take on more of that responsibility. And now I feel like I’m right on the edge of losing that chance because I keep making mistakes when things get busy, or I get confused during closing. It’s not just embarrassing little mistakes, either — it slows everyone down. If I mess up closing checks or I don’t follow through on what I’m supposed to do, somebody else has to stop and fix it, and then service gets backed up, and people get irritated.

I can tell my manager doesn’t look at me the same way. Like, they’re not saying, “You can’t handle this,” directly, but I can feel it in how they hesitate before giving me tasks they used to trust me with. And that really bothers me because I used to be dependable there. I liked being good at my job. Now I feel like I’m becoming the person people have to work around.

And at home, yeah, it’s affecting things too. My husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’ll be sitting there with him, but he says it’s like I’m not really there, not really listening or responding the way I used to. I don’t mean to be like that. I’m not trying to ignore him. But I can tell it’s wearing on him, and then I feel guilty because work is already falling apart and then I come home and I’m not really giving him much either.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F43.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work kind of made me come in. I’m a waitress, and things have been getting weird during service, not just like normal stress or being busy. What scared everyone was that I started freezing up in the middle of a shift, like I’d just stop with people waiting and food needing to go out.\n\nAnd I’d do things in the wrong order, too. Like instead of closing out checks, I’d be standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, just repeating table numbers to myself. Table 12, table 7, table 12 again—like that. I knew people were looking at me, and my manager got really worried because it wasn’t just one little mistake. It was like I couldn’t move to the next step right. So… they told me I should talk to someone, and honestly it scared me too.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I’ve been upset and embarrassed about what’s been happening, and kind of scared because I don’t like feeling like I’m not acting like myself at work. But “sad or down” like… crying all day or feeling heartbroken or something, I’m not sure that’s it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m tense and confused, maybe frustrated. I do have moments where I sit there afterward and think, “What is wrong with me?” and that makes me feel pretty low, but I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body watching yourself during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had it exactly like… floating above myself or watching myself like I’m in a movie, if that’s what you mean. During those moments it’s more like I’m stuck or confused, and afterward I’m trying to piece together why I was standing there doing nothing.\n\nI do feel kind of unreal sometimes in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts. Like everything feels too loud or too sharp, and I’m aware people are around me, but I’m not really following what I’m supposed to do next. But outside my body specifically? I don’t think I can say yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you remember everything that happens during these episodes, or are there parts that you don't recall later?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t think it’s like a complete blackout, like I wake up somewhere and have no idea how I got there. It’s not that dramatic. I usually remember being there, like I remember the restaurant, the noise, people moving around, my manager’s face, that kind of thing.\n\nBut the order of it gets… fuzzy. Like I can remember pieces, but not always exactly what happened first or how long I was standing there. Sometimes afterward someone will say, “You were just holding that and staring,” or “I asked you three times,” and I don’t remember it that clearly. Or I remember them talking, but not the actual words, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like gaps in the details than losing the whole episode. I’ll know I was confused, and I’ll remember feeling stuck, but when I try to explain it later, I’m sort of filling in from what people tell me and from little flashes of what I noticed at the time. So… I guess parts are kind of blurry, but I don’t know if that counts as not recalling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I guess it depends what you mean by disconnected. I don’t feel like I disappear or anything, and I don’t think I’m watching myself from somewhere else. But in the moment, things can feel… off. Like I’m there, but I’m not fully plugged into what’s happening.\n\nSometimes it’s like the room keeps going around me, people are talking and moving and expecting me to respond, but I’m sort of behind a pane of glass or a step too slow. I can see what’s around me, but it doesn’t quite feel normal, and I don’t always feel like I’m matching up with what I’m supposed to be doing. I’ll be holding something or standing there, and part of me knows I should move, but it doesn’t connect right away.\n\nSo, disconnected from myself? Maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly. Disconnected from what’s going on around me, maybe more so. It’s just hard to describe without making it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things around you are not real during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’re not real, exactly. It’s more like they feel strange, or kind of distant, even though I know I’m in the room and I know the tables and people and noises are actually there.\n\nLike, I don’t usually think, “This isn’t real,” in a clear way. It’s not like I believe the restaurant disappears or anything. But everything can feel off, like the lights are too bright or the sounds are too much, and I’m looking at things but not really taking them in normally. Almost like when you’re really tired and everything feels kind of flat or separate from you, except it happens when I’m supposed to be working and responding.\n\nSo… maybe a little unreal, but not completely unreal. I still know where I am, I just don’t feel like I’m connecting to it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I get stressed like anybody does, especially at work or if I’m running late or there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know if I’d call it being anxious all the time or constantly on edge.\n\nWhen I’m not having one of those moments, I can still feel a little keyed up sometimes, like I’m kind of watching myself to see if I’m going to mess up or if something weird is going to happen, but I don’t know how often that is compared to normal stress. It’s hard to separate, because restaurants are already loud and rushed and people want things from you every second, so sometimes I think, “Well, of course I’m tense.”\n\nBut if you mean am I sitting around every day feeling panicky or scared for no reason, I don’t think so. Not usually. I have times where I feel fine, or at least normal for me. So… maybe some stress, maybe some nervousness, but I haven’t really noticed it as its own separate thing the way I notice the episodes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to say yes or no like I know for sure.\n\nWith appetite, I think it’s mostly just… normal? Some days at work I forget to eat because it gets busy, or I’ll just pick at something late, but that’s kind of always been how restaurant shifts go for me. I haven’t noticed a big change where food suddenly seems disgusting or I’m starving all the time or anything like that.\n\nSleep is harder to answer because my schedule can be weird, and if I get home late it takes me a while to settle down. But I don’t know if that’s new or if it’s just my routine being inconsistent. I might have a few nights where I don’t sleep great, then I’ll crash more on another night. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern recently, I guess. If you want, I can try to pay more attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of worthlessness or guilt about yourself lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around thinking, “I’m worthless,” in those exact words. That feels too strong, maybe. I do get frustrated with myself sometimes, especially if I feel like I’m not handling things the way I should or if I’m slower than other people at work, but I don’t know if that’s guilt or just being annoyed with myself.\n\nGuilt… I mean, maybe in small ways? Like if I mess something up, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why can’t I just be normal about things?” But I don’t think I’ve had this big heavy feeling that I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more like self-criticism, I guess.\n\nSo… not really, not lately in a major way. I haven’t noticed worthlessness or guilt as a big thing on its own.\n\nDoctor: Has something very stressful or upsetting happened to you recently, like the loss of a loved one or a traumatic experience?\n\nPatient: Um… not that I can think of. I mean, nothing like someone close to me dying or an accident or being attacked or anything like that. I’ve had stressful days, definitely, and things can build up, especially with work and normal life stuff, but I don’t think there’s been one big event where I’d say, “That’s what set everything off.”\n\nI keep trying to think if I’m forgetting something, because sometimes when people ask that I feel like maybe there’s supposed to be an answer, but… no, I don’t think so. Nothing major or traumatic recently. Just regular stress, I guess, not something like a loss or a specific terrible thing happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so? Like, I’ve heard people describe that as feeling like they’re floating above themselves or watching themselves like a movie, and I don’t really recognize that, at least not clearly.\n\nSometimes when I’m stressed or tired I can feel kind of disconnected, like I’m going through the motions or not fully paying attention, but I wouldn’t say I’m outside my body looking at myself. It’s more like I’m in my own head too much, if that makes sense. I’m still aware I’m there, I’m still the one doing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that specific feeling. If you mean something more subtle than that, maybe I’d need you to explain what you’re asking, because I might be misunderstanding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I do worry, but I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a way that stands out. Like, I worry about normal things—whether I’m going to mess something up at work, or if I said something weird, or if I forgot to do something—but it’s hard for me to tell what’s normal worry versus something bigger.\n\nWhen I’m not having one of those episodes, I can usually sort of keep going. The worry might be in the background, like a little noise, but it’s not always right in my face. Some days I’m calmer, and some days I get stuck thinking through things too much. I’ll replay conversations or think ahead about what could go wrong, but I don’t know if that’s “often” or just me being kind of cautious.\n\nSo… I guess yes, sometimes I worry when I’m not in one of those episodes, but I haven’t really kept track of it. It’s not like I wake up every day thinking, “I’m worried,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the time, no. I mean, I get anxious, but it’s hard for me to measure it like that. If I’m at work and it’s busy, or I feel like someone is annoyed with me, then yeah, I can get tense and start worrying. And sometimes even when nothing is actually happening, I’ll have this background feeling like I should be paying attention to something, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nBut if you mean every day, all day, like I’m constantly anxious even when nothing else is going on… I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly. There are times where I feel okay, or at least distracted enough that I’m not really noticing anxiety. Then other times I’ll realize I’ve been clenching my jaw or overthinking something small for way too long.\n\nSo… I guess I do feel anxious sometimes outside of the episodes, but I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having another one of these episodes happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about that. Maybe not constantly in a neat way, like “I’m afraid of an episode,” but I worry about it happening at work again because that’s where it got really embarrassing and scary for other people.\n\nWhat scared everyone was I started sort of freezing up in the middle of service. Like I’d be standing there when I was supposed to be closing checks or running drinks, and I just… wouldn’t move on to the next thing right. Or I’d do things out of order. One time I was by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and instead of going to close checks, I was just repeating table numbers to myself. I knew they were table numbers, but I wasn’t doing what I was supposed to be doing with them, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd in a restaurant, everything is so fast, so if you stop or get mixed up, people notice immediately. Servers are asking where the key is, customers are waiting, managers are looking at you like, “What are you doing?” So yes, I worry about it happening again, especially when we’re busy, because I don’t always feel like I’ll catch it before other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a dream or detached from the world around you when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t know if I’d call it feeling like I’m in a dream, exactly. It’s more like… things don’t line up right in my head for a while, and I’m aware people are around me, but I’m not connecting to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike, if you mean do I look around and think, “This isn’t real,” I don’t think so. I don’t remember thinking that in a clear way. But I can feel kind of far away from what’s happening, like there’s a delay between what I see and what I’m supposed to do next. People might be talking to me, and I hear them, but it doesn’t always land the way it should.\n\nSo maybe detached a little, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as being in a dream. It’s not like I’m floating above myself or anything dramatic like that. It’s more confusing and stuck, like I’m there, but not fully keeping up with the room.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes been affecting your relationships with coworkers or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because I keep wanting to say it’s just work stress or people are overreacting, but it’s not just that.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten to the point where I’m pretty sure I’m close to losing the shift-lead opportunity they were talking to me about. Before all this, my manager had been kind of testing me out with more responsibility, like helping with closing and keeping things moving during service. And now it feels like every time I make one of those mistakes, or I get confused and hold people up, it becomes this reason not to trust me with it.\n\nIt’s not even one big dramatic thing every time. It’s little things that really matter in a restaurant — closing checks wrong, slowing down service, forgetting what step I’m supposed to do next, getting stuck when everyone else needs me to move faster. Then other people have to cover for me, or someone has to come behind me and fix something, and I can tell they’re frustrated. They don’t always say it directly, but you can feel it. Like people start watching you differently.\n\nWith coworkers, I think some of them are still nice to me, but it’s awkward now. I feel like I’ve become someone they have to manage around instead of rely on. And that hurts because I used to be good at that job. I liked being the person who could handle a rush.\n\nOutside of work… mostly it’s my husband. He says I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’ll be sitting there at home, but not really with him, if that makes sense. He’ll tell me about his day or ask me something, and I guess I don’t respond right away, or I seem like I’m somewhere else. He’s been patient, but I can tell he’s tired and lonely in it. And then I feel guilty, because I’m physically there, but I’m not giving him much of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not in control of your actions during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not exactly like somebody else is controlling me, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I’m not controlling myself the way I normally would. Like my body is there and I’m holding the thing I need, but I’m not doing the next step.\n\nAt work, that’s what scared people, I think. I’d freeze in the middle of service when everything was moving fast, or I’d start doing things in the wrong order. I remember standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and I was supposed to be using it to close checks, but instead I was just repeating table numbers to myself. I knew they were table numbers, and I knew I was at work, but I wasn’t making myself move on to what I was supposed to do.\n\nSo it’s not like I black out and wake up somewhere else. It’s more embarrassing than that, almost, because I’m partly aware that I’m messing up, but I can’t seem to organize myself into the right action fast enough. Like I get stuck in one step while everyone else is already three steps ahead.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your personal or work life because of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, definitely. That’s honestly the part that makes it harder to keep telling myself it’s just me being tired or having a bad week.\n\nAt work it’s been a real problem. I’m a waitress, and before this got worse, they were talking about maybe moving me toward a shift-lead kind of role, or at least letting me take on more of that responsibility. And now I feel like I’m right on the edge of losing that chance because I keep making mistakes when things get busy, or I get confused during closing. It’s not just embarrassing little mistakes, either — it slows everyone down. If I mess up closing checks or I don’t follow through on what I’m supposed to do, somebody else has to stop and fix it, and then service gets backed up, and people get irritated.\n\nI can tell my manager doesn’t look at me the same way. Like, they’re not saying, “You can’t handle this,” directly, but I can feel it in how they hesitate before giving me tasks they used to trust me with. And that really bothers me because I used to be dependable there. I liked being good at my job. Now I feel like I’m becoming the person people have to work around.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s affecting things too. My husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’ll be sitting there with him, but he says it’s like I’m not really there, not really listening or responding the way I used to. I don’t mean to be like that. I’m not trying to ignore him. But I can tell it’s wearing on him, and then I feel guilty because work is already falling apart and then I come home and I’m not really giving him much either.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s more like I’m in my own head too much, if that makes sense. I’m still aware I’m there, I’m still the one doing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that specific feeling. If you mean something more subtle than that, maybe I’d need you to explain what you’re asking, because I might be misunderstanding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I do worry, but I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a way that stands out. Like, I worry about normal things—whether I’m going to mess something up at work, or if I said something weird, or if I forgot to do something—but it’s hard for me to tell what’s normal worry versus something bigger.\n\nWhen I’m not having one of those episodes, I can usually sort of keep going. The worry might be in the background, like a little noise, but it’s not always right in my face. Some days I’m calmer, and some days I get stuck thinking through things too much. I’ll replay conversations or think ahead about what could go wrong, but I don’t know if that’s “often” or just me being kind of cautious.\n\nSo… I guess yes, sometimes I worry when I’m not in one of those episodes, but I haven’t really kept track of it. It’s not like I wake up every day thinking, “I’m worried,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the time, no. I mean, I get anxious, but it’s hard for me to measure it like that. If I’m at work and it’s busy, or I feel like someone is annoyed with me, then yeah, I can get tense and start worrying. And sometimes even when nothing is actually happening, I’ll have this background feeling like I should be paying attention to something, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nBut if you mean every day, all day, like I’m constantly anxious even when nothing else is going on… I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly. There are times where I feel okay, or at least distracted enough that I’m not really noticing anxiety. Then other times I’ll realize I’ve been clenching my jaw or overthinking something small for way too long.\n\nSo… I guess I do feel anxious sometimes outside of the episodes, but I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having another one of these episodes happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about that. Maybe not constantly in a neat way, like “I’m afraid of an episode,” but I worry about it happening at work again because that’s where it got really embarrassing and scary for other people.\n\nWhat scared everyone was I started sort of freezing up in the middle of service. Like I’d be standing there when I was supposed to be closing checks or running drinks, and I just… wouldn’t move on to the next thing right. Or I’d do things out of order. One time I was by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and instead of going to close checks, I was just repeating table numbers to myself. I knew they were table numbers, but I wasn’t doing what I was supposed to be doing with them, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd in a restaurant, everything is so fast, so if you stop or get mixed up, people notice immediately. Servers are asking where the key is, customers are waiting, managers are looking at you like, “What are you doing?” So yes, I worry about it happening again, especially when we’re busy, because I don’t always feel like I’ll catch it before other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a dream or detached from the world around you when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t know if I’d call it feeling like I’m in a dream, exactly. It’s more like… things don’t line up right in my head for a while, and I’m aware people are around me, but I’m not connecting to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike, if you mean do I look around and think, “This isn’t real,” I don’t think so. I don’t remember thinking that in a clear way. But I can feel kind of far away from what’s happening, like there’s a delay between what I see and what I’m supposed to do next. People might be talking to me, and I hear them, but it doesn’t always land the way it should.\n\nSo maybe detached a little, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as being in a dream. It’s not like I’m floating above myself or anything dramatic like that. It’s more confusing and stuck, like I’m there, but not fully keeping up with the room.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes been affecting your relationships with coworkers or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because I keep wanting to say it’s just work stress or people are overreacting, but it’s not just that.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten to the point where I’m pretty sure I’m close to losing the shift-lead opportunity they were talking to me about. Before all this, my manager had been kind of testing me out with more responsibility, like helping with closing and keeping things moving during service. And now it feels like every time I make one of those mistakes, or I get confused and hold people up, it becomes this reason not to trust me with it.\n\nIt’s not even one big dramatic thing every time. It’s little things that really matter in a restaurant — closing checks wrong, slowing down service, forgetting what step I’m supposed to do next, getting stuck when everyone else needs me to move faster. Then other people have to cover for me, or someone has to come behind me and fix something, and I can tell they’re frustrated. They don’t always say it directly, but you can feel it. Like people start watching you differently.\n\nWith coworkers, I think some of them are still nice to me, but it’s awkward now. I feel like I’ve become someone they have to manage around instead of rely on. And that hurts because I used to be good at that job. I liked being the person who could handle a rush.\n\nOutside of work… mostly it’s my husband. He says I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’ll be sitting there at home, but not really with him, if that makes sense. He’ll tell me about his day or ask me something, and I guess I don’t respond right away, or I seem like I’m somewhere else. He’s been patient, but I can tell he’s tired and lonely in it. And then I feel guilty, because I’m physically there, but I’m not giving him much of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not in control of your actions during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not exactly like somebody else is controlling me, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I’m not controlling myself the way I normally would. Like my body is there and I’m holding the thing I need, but I’m not doing the next step.\n\nAt work, that’s what scared people, I think. I’d freeze in the middle of service when everything was moving fast, or I’d start doing things in the wrong order. I remember standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and I was supposed to be using it to close checks, but instead I was just repeating table numbers to myself. I knew they were table numbers, and I knew I was at work, but I wasn’t making myself move on to what I was supposed to do.\n\nSo it’s not like I black out and wake up somewhere else. It’s more embarrassing than that, almost, because I’m partly aware that I’m messing up, but I can’t seem to organize myself into the right action fast enough. Like I get stuck in one step while everyone else is already three steps ahead.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your personal or work life because of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, definitely. That’s honestly the part that makes it harder to keep telling myself it’s just me being tired or having a bad week.\n\nAt work it’s been a real problem. I’m a waitress, and before this got worse, they were talking about maybe moving me toward a shift-lead kind of role, or at least letting me take on more of that responsibility. And now I feel like I’m right on the edge of losing that chance because I keep making mistakes when things get busy, or I get confused during closing. It’s not just embarrassing little mistakes, either — it slows everyone down. If I mess up closing checks or I don’t follow through on what I’m supposed to do, somebody else has to stop and fix it, and then service gets backed up, and people get irritated.\n\nI can tell my manager doesn’t look at me the same way. Like, they’re not saying, “You can’t handle this,” directly, but I can feel it in how they hesitate before giving me tasks they used to trust me with. And that really bothers me because I used to be dependable there. I liked being good at my job. Now I feel like I’m becoming the person people have to work around.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s affecting things too. My husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’ll be sitting there with him, but he says it’s like I’m not really there, not really listening or responding the way I used to. I don’t mean to be like that. I’m not trying to ignore him. But I can tell it’s wearing on him, and then I feel guilty because work is already falling apart and then I come home and I’m not really giving him much either.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yes. I mean, I don’t really know how else to answer that except yes, it’s interfering a lot.

At work especially, it’s gotten bad enough that I’m worried I’m going to lose that shift-lead opportunity they were considering me for. And at home, it’s not like everything is normal once I leave work. My husband keeps saying I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’m there physically, but I’m not really with him the way I used to be. So yeah… it’s definitely affecting my daily life, not just in my head.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F43.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work kind of made me come in. I’m a waitress, and things have been getting weird during service, not just like normal stress or being busy. What scared everyone was that I started freezing up in the middle of a shift, like I’d just stop with people waiting and food needing to go out.\n\nAnd I’d do things in the wrong order, too. Like instead of closing out checks, I’d be standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, just repeating table numbers to myself. Table 12, table 7, table 12 again—like that. I knew people were looking at me, and my manager got really worried because it wasn’t just one little mistake. It was like I couldn’t move to the next step right. So… they told me I should talk to someone, and honestly it scared me too.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I’ve been upset and embarrassed about what’s been happening, and kind of scared because I don’t like feeling like I’m not acting like myself at work. But “sad or down” like… crying all day or feeling heartbroken or something, I’m not sure that’s it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m tense and confused, maybe frustrated. I do have moments where I sit there afterward and think, “What is wrong with me?” and that makes me feel pretty low, but I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body watching yourself during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had it exactly like… floating above myself or watching myself like I’m in a movie, if that’s what you mean. During those moments it’s more like I’m stuck or confused, and afterward I’m trying to piece together why I was standing there doing nothing.\n\nI do feel kind of unreal sometimes in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts. Like everything feels too loud or too sharp, and I’m aware people are around me, but I’m not really following what I’m supposed to do next. But outside my body specifically? I don’t think I can say yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you remember everything that happens during these episodes, or are there parts that you don't recall later?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t think it’s like a complete blackout, like I wake up somewhere and have no idea how I got there. It’s not that dramatic. I usually remember being there, like I remember the restaurant, the noise, people moving around, my manager’s face, that kind of thing.\n\nBut the order of it gets… fuzzy. Like I can remember pieces, but not always exactly what happened first or how long I was standing there. Sometimes afterward someone will say, “You were just holding that and staring,” or “I asked you three times,” and I don’t remember it that clearly. Or I remember them talking, but not the actual words, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like gaps in the details than losing the whole episode. I’ll know I was confused, and I’ll remember feeling stuck, but when I try to explain it later, I’m sort of filling in from what people tell me and from little flashes of what I noticed at the time. So… I guess parts are kind of blurry, but I don’t know if that counts as not recalling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I guess it depends what you mean by disconnected. I don’t feel like I disappear or anything, and I don’t think I’m watching myself from somewhere else. But in the moment, things can feel… off. Like I’m there, but I’m not fully plugged into what’s happening.\n\nSometimes it’s like the room keeps going around me, people are talking and moving and expecting me to respond, but I’m sort of behind a pane of glass or a step too slow. I can see what’s around me, but it doesn’t quite feel normal, and I don’t always feel like I’m matching up with what I’m supposed to be doing. I’ll be holding something or standing there, and part of me knows I should move, but it doesn’t connect right away.\n\nSo, disconnected from myself? Maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly. Disconnected from what’s going on around me, maybe more so. It’s just hard to describe without making it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things around you are not real during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’re not real, exactly. It’s more like they feel strange, or kind of distant, even though I know I’m in the room and I know the tables and people and noises are actually there.\n\nLike, I don’t usually think, “This isn’t real,” in a clear way. It’s not like I believe the restaurant disappears or anything. But everything can feel off, like the lights are too bright or the sounds are too much, and I’m looking at things but not really taking them in normally. Almost like when you’re really tired and everything feels kind of flat or separate from you, except it happens when I’m supposed to be working and responding.\n\nSo… maybe a little unreal, but not completely unreal. I still know where I am, I just don’t feel like I’m connecting to it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I get stressed like anybody does, especially at work or if I’m running late or there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know if I’d call it being anxious all the time or constantly on edge.\n\nWhen I’m not having one of those moments, I can still feel a little keyed up sometimes, like I’m kind of watching myself to see if I’m going to mess up or if something weird is going to happen, but I don’t know how often that is compared to normal stress. It’s hard to separate, because restaurants are already loud and rushed and people want things from you every second, so sometimes I think, “Well, of course I’m tense.”\n\nBut if you mean am I sitting around every day feeling panicky or scared for no reason, I don’t think so. Not usually. I have times where I feel fine, or at least normal for me. So… maybe some stress, maybe some nervousness, but I haven’t really noticed it as its own separate thing the way I notice the episodes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to say yes or no like I know for sure.\n\nWith appetite, I think it’s mostly just… normal? Some days at work I forget to eat because it gets busy, or I’ll just pick at something late, but that’s kind of always been how restaurant shifts go for me. I haven’t noticed a big change where food suddenly seems disgusting or I’m starving all the time or anything like that.\n\nSleep is harder to answer because my schedule can be weird, and if I get home late it takes me a while to settle down. But I don’t know if that’s new or if it’s just my routine being inconsistent. I might have a few nights where I don’t sleep great, then I’ll crash more on another night. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern recently, I guess. If you want, I can try to pay more attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of worthlessness or guilt about yourself lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around thinking, “I’m worthless,” in those exact words. That feels too strong, maybe. I do get frustrated with myself sometimes, especially if I feel like I’m not handling things the way I should or if I’m slower than other people at work, but I don’t know if that’s guilt or just being annoyed with myself.\n\nGuilt… I mean, maybe in small ways? Like if I mess something up, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to, like, “Why did I do that?” or “Why can’t I just be normal about things?” But I don’t think I’ve had this big heavy feeling that I’m a bad person or that everything is my fault. It’s more like self-criticism, I guess.\n\nSo… not really, not lately in a major way. I haven’t noticed worthlessness or guilt as a big thing on its own.\n\nDoctor: Has something very stressful or upsetting happened to you recently, like the loss of a loved one or a traumatic experience?\n\nPatient: Um… not that I can think of. I mean, nothing like someone close to me dying or an accident or being attacked or anything like that. I’ve had stressful days, definitely, and things can build up, especially with work and normal life stuff, but I don’t think there’s been one big event where I’d say, “That’s what set everything off.”\n\nI keep trying to think if I’m forgetting something, because sometimes when people ask that I feel like maybe there’s supposed to be an answer, but… no, I don’t think so. Nothing major or traumatic recently. Just regular stress, I guess, not something like a loss or a specific terrible thing happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so? Like, I’ve heard people describe that as feeling like they’re floating above themselves or watching themselves like a movie, and I don’t really recognize that, at least not clearly.\n\nSometimes when I’m stressed or tired I can feel kind of disconnected, like I’m going through the motions or not fully paying attention, but I wouldn’t say I’m outside my body looking at myself. It’s more like I’m in my own head too much, if that makes sense. I’m still aware I’m there, I’m still the one doing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that specific feeling. If you mean something more subtle than that, maybe I’d need you to explain what you’re asking, because I might be misunderstanding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I do worry, but I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a way that stands out. Like, I worry about normal things—whether I’m going to mess something up at work, or if I said something weird, or if I forgot to do something—but it’s hard for me to tell what’s normal worry versus something bigger.\n\nWhen I’m not having one of those episodes, I can usually sort of keep going. The worry might be in the background, like a little noise, but it’s not always right in my face. Some days I’m calmer, and some days I get stuck thinking through things too much. I’ll replay conversations or think ahead about what could go wrong, but I don’t know if that’s “often” or just me being kind of cautious.\n\nSo… I guess yes, sometimes I worry when I’m not in one of those episodes, but I haven’t really kept track of it. It’s not like I wake up every day thinking, “I’m worried,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the time, no. I mean, I get anxious, but it’s hard for me to measure it like that. If I’m at work and it’s busy, or I feel like someone is annoyed with me, then yeah, I can get tense and start worrying. And sometimes even when nothing is actually happening, I’ll have this background feeling like I should be paying attention to something, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nBut if you mean every day, all day, like I’m constantly anxious even when nothing else is going on… I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly. There are times where I feel okay, or at least distracted enough that I’m not really noticing anxiety. Then other times I’ll realize I’ve been clenching my jaw or overthinking something small for way too long.\n\nSo… I guess I do feel anxious sometimes outside of the episodes, but I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having another one of these episodes happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about that. Maybe not constantly in a neat way, like “I’m afraid of an episode,” but I worry about it happening at work again because that’s where it got really embarrassing and scary for other people.\n\nWhat scared everyone was I started sort of freezing up in the middle of service. Like I’d be standing there when I was supposed to be closing checks or running drinks, and I just… wouldn’t move on to the next thing right. Or I’d do things out of order. One time I was by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and instead of going to close checks, I was just repeating table numbers to myself. I knew they were table numbers, but I wasn’t doing what I was supposed to be doing with them, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd in a restaurant, everything is so fast, so if you stop or get mixed up, people notice immediately. Servers are asking where the key is, customers are waiting, managers are looking at you like, “What are you doing?” So yes, I worry about it happening again, especially when we’re busy, because I don’t always feel like I’ll catch it before other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a dream or detached from the world around you when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t know if I’d call it feeling like I’m in a dream, exactly. It’s more like… things don’t line up right in my head for a while, and I’m aware people are around me, but I’m not connecting to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike, if you mean do I look around and think, “This isn’t real,” I don’t think so. I don’t remember thinking that in a clear way. But I can feel kind of far away from what’s happening, like there’s a delay between what I see and what I’m supposed to do next. People might be talking to me, and I hear them, but it doesn’t always land the way it should.\n\nSo maybe detached a little, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as being in a dream. It’s not like I’m floating above myself or anything dramatic like that. It’s more confusing and stuck, like I’m there, but not fully keeping up with the room.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes been affecting your relationships with coworkers or friends outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, because I keep wanting to say it’s just work stress or people are overreacting, but it’s not just that.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten to the point where I’m pretty sure I’m close to losing the shift-lead opportunity they were talking to me about. Before all this, my manager had been kind of testing me out with more responsibility, like helping with closing and keeping things moving during service. And now it feels like every time I make one of those mistakes, or I get confused and hold people up, it becomes this reason not to trust me with it.\n\nIt’s not even one big dramatic thing every time. It’s little things that really matter in a restaurant — closing checks wrong, slowing down service, forgetting what step I’m supposed to do next, getting stuck when everyone else needs me to move faster. Then other people have to cover for me, or someone has to come behind me and fix something, and I can tell they’re frustrated. They don’t always say it directly, but you can feel it. Like people start watching you differently.\n\nWith coworkers, I think some of them are still nice to me, but it’s awkward now. I feel like I’ve become someone they have to manage around instead of rely on. And that hurts because I used to be good at that job. I liked being the person who could handle a rush.\n\nOutside of work… mostly it’s my husband. He says I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’ll be sitting there at home, but not really with him, if that makes sense. He’ll tell me about his day or ask me something, and I guess I don’t respond right away, or I seem like I’m somewhere else. He’s been patient, but I can tell he’s tired and lonely in it. And then I feel guilty, because I’m physically there, but I’m not giving him much of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're not in control of your actions during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not exactly like somebody else is controlling me, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I’m not controlling myself the way I normally would. Like my body is there and I’m holding the thing I need, but I’m not doing the next step.\n\nAt work, that’s what scared people, I think. I’d freeze in the middle of service when everything was moving fast, or I’d start doing things in the wrong order. I remember standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and I was supposed to be using it to close checks, but instead I was just repeating table numbers to myself. I knew they were table numbers, and I knew I was at work, but I wasn’t making myself move on to what I was supposed to do.\n\nSo it’s not like I black out and wake up somewhere else. It’s more embarrassing than that, almost, because I’m partly aware that I’m messing up, but I can’t seem to organize myself into the right action fast enough. Like I get stuck in one step while everyone else is already three steps ahead.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your personal or work life because of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, definitely. That’s honestly the part that makes it harder to keep telling myself it’s just me being tired or having a bad week.\n\nAt work it’s been a real problem. I’m a waitress, and before this got worse, they were talking about maybe moving me toward a shift-lead kind of role, or at least letting me take on more of that responsibility. And now I feel like I’m right on the edge of losing that chance because I keep making mistakes when things get busy, or I get confused during closing. It’s not just embarrassing little mistakes, either — it slows everyone down. If I mess up closing checks or I don’t follow through on what I’m supposed to do, somebody else has to stop and fix it, and then service gets backed up, and people get irritated.\n\nI can tell my manager doesn’t look at me the same way. Like, they’re not saying, “You can’t handle this,” directly, but I can feel it in how they hesitate before giving me tasks they used to trust me with. And that really bothers me because I used to be dependable there. I liked being good at my job. Now I feel like I’m becoming the person people have to work around.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s affecting things too. My husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’ll be sitting there with him, but he says it’s like I’m not really there, not really listening or responding the way I used to. I don’t mean to be like that. I’m not trying to ignore him. But I can tell it’s wearing on him, and then I feel guilty because work is already falling apart and then I come home and I’m not really giving him much either.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these episodes are significantly interfering with your ability to function normally in your daily life or at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t really know how else to answer that except yes, it’s interfering a lot.\n\nAt work especially, it’s gotten bad enough that I’m worried I’m going to lose that shift-lead opportunity they were considering me for. And I know I’ve said that, but it’s because it’s kind of the clearest example of how this isn’t just “I feel off.” It’s actually changing what people think I can handle. I’ll make mistakes or get confused during service or closing, and then it doesn’t just affect me — it backs other people up, or someone has to fix what I did, or they stop trusting me to do the next thing.\n\nAnd at home, it’s not like everything is normal once I leave work. My husband keeps saying I’m barely present with him anymore. Like I’m there physically, but I’m not really with him the way I used to be. So yeah… it’s definitely affecting my daily life, not just in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
